- From X or K you must go to F or back to G (no other way forward). Thus, all routes from G inevitably pass through either F or H (since G→X/K must go to F to proceed; G→H is the other branch).
- From F, there is a single onward neighbor toward the middle: E (other neighbors are only X, K, G). So F→E is the only way from the X/K/F side into the core.
- From H, onward neighbors are B and I (and G). So the H-branch enters the core via either B or I.

Now examine routes from the core to N:
- To reach N, the last step must be through B, C, O, or Y.
- Y only connects to O and N; to reach Y you must pass through O. Therefore entering N via Y is still via O.
- So effectively, endpoints are {B, C, O}.

Enumerate independent channels from G to N:
1) Through F→E and then down to B (since E connects to B). Path example: G-F-E-B-N.
2) Through F→E and then to C (E connects to C via edge E–C). Example: G-F-E-C-N.
3) Through H→B directly: G-H-B-N.
4) Through H→I and then to O (I connects to O via I–O path through P or directly? I connects to O via edge I–O; also I–P and I–J edges exist). Example: G-H-I-O-N.
5) Through H→I and then to C (I connects to C). Example: G-H-I-C-N.
6) Through H→I and via P/J/D subgraph to O also possible, but all still funnel to O.

Observe cut candidates:
- If we remove E, all routes using F are broken (since F’s only forward link is to E).
- If we remove I and B together, H-branch would be blocked; but maybe a smaller single node can block both H→B and H→I branches? Removing H itself would sever all G paths via H, but we are allowed to remove H. However, we must also ensure paths via F are blocked; removing only H leaves F→E routes intact. So we need one node to kill F-branch (E), and one node to kill H-branch.
- What single node kills H-branch completely? Candidates:
  - H itself (cuts both G→H and any H onward). That works.
  - Downstream combined node that all H-branch paths must pass through does not exist because H has two disjoint exits B and I that rejoin later only near N at {B,C,O}. No single node among {B, I, C, O} is shared by all H-branch paths: paths can go via B directly to N, avoiding I/C/O; or via I→O/C, avoiding B. So one node cannot block both unless we remove H.
- Is there any path from G to N that bypasses both H and F? From G the only neighbors are X, K, F, H. From X/K you must go to F. So no bypass. Therefore removing H blocks the H-side; removing E blocks the F-side.

Verify minimality:
- One node is insufficient: If we remove E only, G→H→B→N remains. If we remove H only, G→F→E→(B or C)→N remains. Any other single node similarly leaves a path via the other branch.
- Two nodes suffice: Removing {E, H} severs all routes because:
  - All paths starting G→F must pass through E; with E removed, F-branch is dead.
  - All paths starting G→H are killed at H itself; with H removed, no connection from G to core via H.

Therefore, the minimum cut set has size 2.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, H]

- Remove {O, J}: Then
  - Y-way blocked at O; B-way blocked at J (outer to P needs J). Remaining: N–C–I–P. Not enough.
- Remove {I, J}: Remaining Y–O–P; not enough.
- Remove {O, I}: Remaining B–H–outer–J–P; not enough.

Hence need 3 nodes. A minimal 3-node cut is to remove one from each essential channel:
- Channel via C/I: remove C or I.
- Channel via Y/O: remove Y or O.
- Channel via B/outer/J: remove B or J.

One example: remove {C, Y, J}.
Verify:
- N–C–… paths blocked at C.
- N–Y–… paths blocked at Y.
- B-route N–B–H–outer–J–P blocked at J; no other edge from outer to P, so this channel closed.
No alternate sneaky route remains from N to P: with C and Y gone, N has only B as neighbor; from B you can go to H, E, etc., but to reach P you’d need J (removed) or go to I/O via C/Y (both blocked). Therefore disconnected.

No size-2 set can work as shown above by exhausting combinations.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [C, Y, J] (other minimal cuts include any one choice from each channel, e.g., [I, O, J] or [C, O, J], etc.)

Check bottlenecks:
- From J: J connects to M and P. From there, many routes head toward the central area (P–O–N–B/E; also P–C–…–B/E; and via M’s long outer path to E). Thus, cutting a single internal node won’t stop all J-based routes unless we block at the F-side neighborhood.
- From P: P connects to D, J, O, and C; from O to N/B and to C/Y; from C to N/B and further to E; so again multiple routes to {B, E}.

Observe that every route from the left (around D, J, P, C, O, M, I, H) to F ultimately must enter through one of F’s four neighbors: G, K, B, or E. If we remove both B and E, paths could still reach F via G or K through the lower-right subgraph. If we remove both G and K, paths could still reach F via B or E through the upper routes. Therefore, to disconnect D from F we must block at least one node from the set {B, E} and at least one node from the set {G, K}.

Minimality:
- Try removing one node only: removing any single node (B, E, G, or K) leaves clear alternate routes via the other three neighbors, so one node is insufficient.
- Removing two nodes:
  - If we remove [B, E], there remain paths D–P–…–H–X/K–G/K–F; so not enough.
  - If we remove [G, K], there remain paths D–P–O–N–B–F or D–…–E–F; so not enough.
  - If we remove [B, G], all upper routes to F via B are cut, and all lower routes to F via G are cut; could K or E still connect? From D side, reaching K requires going through G (edges into K are from G and X and F; but X connects to G and H only; without G, K is isolated from the left), so K cannot be reached. Reaching E is possible from left, but E–F is direct; however with B removed, there are still left-to-E routes (e.g., D–P–O–N–B? no B; D–P–O–…–E directly via E–B–N? There is a direct edge B–E only via the horizontal line; without B, E is still connected to L and to F and to the outer path from M. Crucially, from the left there is a long outer path M–…–E; starting from D one can go D–J–M–outer–E–F. Thus [B, G] fails.
  - Remove [E, G]: From left you can still reach B and then F via B–F; so fails.
  - Remove [B, K]: With B gone, only E on the top remains to reach F; with K gone, the lower entry is only G. Can the left reach G? Yes via H–X–G; then G–F is direct, so path exists; fails.
  - Remove [E, K]: Then lower entry via G still exists (D–…–H–X–G–F). Also upper via B exists; fails.

Therefore, size 2 is not enough.

Consider size 3:
- Remove one from {B, E} and both from {G, K}, e.g., [B, G, K]:
  - Lower entries G and K are gone, so only possible F entries are via E. But B is removed, leaving only E as neighbor on the top. Check if a D–…–E path exists: Yes, D–J–M–outer–E is present; thus E–F gives a path; so need to also remove E, not B.
- Remove [E, G, K]:
  - Now F’s remaining neighbor is only B.
  - Are there D–…–B paths? Yes via D–P–O–N–B or D–P–C–B, etc. Hence still connected.
- Remove [B, E, G]:
  - F’s only remaining neighbor is K. From the left, can we reach K without G? K connects to X, G, and F. Without G, the only access to K from the left is via X, but X only connects to H and G; since G is removed, X becomes a dead end from H. Therefore K is unreachable from D. Since E and B are also removed, no other neighbor of F is reachable. Thus all D–F paths are broken.
  - Verify alternates: Any route from D reaches the lower area through I–H or via central P/C/O, but to touch F it must go through K (only remaining). With G removed, X cannot lead to K (X–K edge exists, but to get to X you go via H–X; yes, H–X exists, so D–…–H–X–K is a path even without G. Wait: X connects to K directly; so K is still reachable via H–X–K even if G is removed. Hence with [B, E, G] removed, there is still a path D–…–H–X–K–F. So this cut fails.

- Remove [B, E, K]:
  - Remaining neighbor of F is only G.
  - Can D reach G? Yes via D–…–H–X–G or via C–…–G; then G–F gives a path. So fails.

Thus 3 nodes chosen as above don’t work. Try [E, G, X]:
  - Remaining neighbors: B and K. From left we can reach B easily; so fail.

To fully isolate F, we must remove both upper neighbors {B, E} and at least one of lower neighbors to block any route to the remaining lower neighbor:
- If we remove B and E, we must also prevent reaching both G and K or at least break the link from left to whichever remains connected to F. But F still connects to both G and K; removing only one of them leaves the other as a direct entry. Therefore with B and E removed, we must remove both G and K as well, totaling 4.

Check [B, E, G, K]:
- With B and E removed, top entries gone.
- With G and K removed, bottom entries gone.
- No other neighbor to F remains; hence no D–F path can exist.
- Minimality: We argued 2 nodes insufficient. We tried several 3-node combinations and any 3-node set leaves at least one neighbor of F reachable from D. Because F has 4 distinct neighbors lying in two largely independent regions, at least 4 removals are required to eliminate all entries.

Try {I, P}: Remaining exits B and E. B–N direct exists, so fails.

- Remove {B, C}: H–…–O–N requires reaching O. H can reach O via I–O or E–M–J–P–O. So still connected.

- Remove {C, O}: H–…–B–N exists. Connected.

- Verify minimality:
  - A single-node removal cannot work:
    • Removing only B leaves O–C–I–(outer frame)–H (e.g., O–C–I–outer rectangle–H).
    • Removing only I leaves O–Y–N–B–H.
  Hence at least two nodes are required, and [B, I] of size 2 works.

- If we remove two nodes among {F, L, M, B}, at least one exit remains, and there is a corresponding route to I. To guarantee disconnection we must remove all four exits or all four entries; but removing four seems large—check if smaller works by mixing.

Consider a mixed minimum vertex cut using separating bottlenecks near I:
- If we remove H and J, paths could still use C or P to reach I (e.g., E–B–C–I, or E–M–J blocked; but E–…–P–I via P remains).
- If we remove C and P, paths via H or J remain (e.g., E–B–H–I or E–M–J–I).
Therefore, at least three removals? Test size 3.
- Try {H, J, C}: path E–…–P–I remains (e.g., E–B–C blocked; but E–F–…–P–I or E–M–J blocked; E–B–H blocked; still via P).
- Try {H, J, P}: path via C remains (E–B–C–I).
- Try {H, C, P}: path via J remains (E–M–J–I).
- Try {J, C, P}: path via H remains (E–B–H–I).
So any 3-node set from {H, J, P, C} fails; hence we must remove all four of them to block all entries to I.

Could we instead block all four exits from E with fewer than four by exploiting a shared bottleneck? Check connections:
- Exits F, L, M, B do not share a single mandatory intermediate node before reaching I; they branch into disjoint areas. For example:
  - F leads to G/H then to I via H or further to P.
  - L leads to M then J to I.
  - M leads directly to J then I.
  - B leads to H or C then I.
No single node (other than the targets) lies on all E→I paths. Any set of three among {F, L, M, B} leaves one exit with a valid route to some entry {H, J, P, C}. Hence cutting all four exits would require 4 removals as well.

Therefore, the minimum cut is size 4 by removing the four neighbors of I (entries): H, J, P, and C. This breaks every E→I path:
- Paths via H are broken.
- Paths via J are broken.
- Paths via C are broken.
- Paths via P are broken.
With these removed, I is isolated from the rest of the graph while E remains intact.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 4, Cut Set: [H, J, P, C]

- If we remove only three edges, any set of three that doesn’t include all of {(E,B), (C,B), (N,B)} leaves at least one route H → … → that neighbor → B.
- Even if (H, B) is removed, there remains at least one of E, C, or N reachable from H, giving a path to B. Hence we also must cut the remaining connection to B by removing all three of {(E,B), (C,B), (N,B)} or equivalently isolating B.

Thus the minimal cut that guarantees disconnection is removing the four edges incident to B that connect it to the rest of the graph.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(H, B), (E, B), (C, B), (N, B)]

Model: gpt-5

--- Model Response ---

List the edges around each:
- H connects to I, C, G.
- E connects to B and F and L.

Now enumerate all H→E paths visible:

From H via I:
1) H–I–(outer path up right)–…–E. Specifically, I connects by a long outer edge to the right side that reaches the segment ending at E. So path: H–I–E (through that continuous outer edge from I to E’s vicinity). Therefore, edge (H, I) carries a direct route toward E.

From H via C:
2) H–C–B–E.
3) H–C–N–B–E.
4) H–C–O–B–E.
5) H–C–O–N–B–E.
6) H–C–(long edge from C to near E via B’s underside)–B–E. All these go through C’s connections to B/N/O.

From H via G:
7) H–G–K–F–E.
8) H–G–X–K–F–E.
9) H–G–F–E (there is a direct G–F edge via the lower-right polygon). Any route from G to E must pass through F, then E.

Identify bottlenecks:
- All routes via G must use edge (F, E) at the end, since F is the only neighbor of E on that lower-right side besides B and L, and L has no link to G’s side. Hence cutting (F, E) blocks every H→…→G→…→F→E path.
- All routes via C (including through N/O/B) must use edge (B, E) at the end, because B is the only neighbor of E on that midline besides F and L, and from C/N/O region there is no connection to F or L without passing B first. Thus cutting (B, E) blocks all H→C→…→B→E paths.
- The path through I reaches E by the outer long edge into E’s area that does not go through B or F. Removing either (H, I) or an appropriate edge on that outer track will stop this unique avenue. The unique edge that makes it an H-connected path is (H, I). Cutting (H, I) severs that route while leaving other parts intact.

Therefore, removing the three edges (B, E), (F, E), and (H, I) blocks:
- all C-region paths via (B, E),
- all G-region paths via (F, E),
- the I-outer path via (H, I).

Minimality check:
- If we omit (B, E), paths via C still reach E: H–C–B–E.
- If we omit (F, E), paths via G still reach E: H–G–K–F–E.
- If we omit (H, I), the I-outer path is gone, but if instead we kept only two cuts (say just (B, E) and (F, E)), there would be no remaining H→E path except the I-outer one; hence at least three edges are required. No single edge can simultaneously block both the C→B route and the G→F route because those converge only at E. Thus the minimum cut size is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, E), (F, E), (H, I)]
